<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Saints Cyril and Methodius" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Saints Cyril and Methodius (disambiguation)">Saints Cyril and Methodius (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

Zograf</a>, 1848, <a href="/wiki/Troyan_Monastery" title="Troyan Monastery">Troyan Monastery</a></div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Bishops and Confessors; Equals to the Apostles; Patrons of Europe; Apostles to the Slavs</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data">826 or 827 and 815<br /><a href="/wiki/Thessalonica" class="mw-redirect" title="Thessalonica">Thessalonica</a>, <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a> (present-day Greece)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data"><span style="display:none">(<span class="dday deathdate">869-02-14</span>)</span>14 February 869 and <span style="display:none">(<span class="dday deathdate">885-04-06</span>)</span>6 April 885<br /><a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> and Velehrad, <a href="/wiki/Great_Moravia" title="Great Moravia">Great Moravia</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a><sup id="cite_ref-teccalendar_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-teccalendar-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><br /><a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheranism</a><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">11 and 24 May<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>)<br /> 14 February (present Roman Catholic calendar); 5 July (Roman Catholic calendar 1880–1886); 7 July (Roman Catholic calendar 1887–1969)<br />5 July (Roman Catholic <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a>)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Brothers depicted together; Eastern bishops holding up a church; Eastern bishops holding an <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Last_Judgment" title="Last Judgment">Last Judgment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-patron_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-patron-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Often, Cyril is depicted wearing a <a href="/wiki/Monastic_habit" class="mw-redirect" title="Monastic habit">monastic habit</a> and Methodius vested as a bishop with <a href="/wiki/Omophorion" title="Omophorion">omophorion</a>.</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia" title="North Macedonia">North Macedonia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Transnistria" title="Transnistria">Transnistria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Ljubljana" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Ljubljana">Archdiocese of Ljubljana</a>, Europe,<sup id="cite_ref-patron_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-patron-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Eparchy_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius_of_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="Eparchy of Saints Cyril and Methodius of Toronto">Slovak Eparchy of Toronto</a>, <a href="/wiki/Slovak_Catholic_Eparchy_of_Ko%C5%A1ice" title="Slovak Catholic Eparchy of Košice">Eparchy of Košice</a><sup id="cite_ref-grkatke-hist_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grkatke-hist-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Cyril</b> (born Constantine, 826–869) and <b>Methodius</b> (815–885) were brothers, <a href="/wiki/Population_of_the_Byzantine_Empire" title="Population of the Byzantine Empire">Byzantine</a> <a href="/wiki/Christian_theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian theologian">Christian theologians</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christian_missionaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian missionaries">missionaries</a>. For their work evangelizing the <a href="/wiki/Slavs" title="Slavs">Slavs</a>, they are known as the "Apostles to the Slavs".<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>They are credited with devising the <a href="/wiki/Glagolitic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Glagolitic alphabet">Glagolitic alphabet</a>, the first alphabet used to transcribe <a href="/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic" title="Old Church Slavonic">Old Church Slavonic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> After their deaths, their pupils continued their missionary work among other Slavs. Both brothers are venerated in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> as <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saints</a> with the title of "<a href="/wiki/Equal-to-apostles" title="Equal-to-apostles">equal-to-apostles</a>". In 1880, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIII" title="Pope Leo XIII">Pope Leo XIII</a> introduced their feast into the calendar of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>. In 1980, the first Slav pope, <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> declared them co-<a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a> of Europe, together with <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia" title="Benedict of Nursia">Benedict of Nursia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Egregiae_Virtutis_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Egregiae_Virtutis-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h3>
<p>The two brothers were born in <a href="/wiki/Thessalonica" class="mw-redirect" title="Thessalonica">Thessalonica</a>, then located in the Byzantine <a href="/wiki/Thessalonica_(theme)" title="Thessalonica (theme)">province of the same name</a> (today in <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a>) – Cyril in about 827–828 and Methodius in about 815–820. According to the <i>Vita Cyrilli</i> ("The Life of Cyril"), Cyril was reputedly the youngest of seven brothers; he was born Constantine,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> but was given the name Cyril upon becoming a monk in Rome shortly before his death.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Methodius was born Michael and was given the name Methodius upon becoming a monk in <a href="/wiki/Polychron" class="mw-redirect" title="Polychron">Polychron</a> Monastery at <a href="/wiki/Mysia" title="Mysia">Mysian</a> Olympus (present-day <a href="/wiki/Uluda%C4%9F" title="Uludağ">Uludağ</a> in northwest <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Their father was Leo, a <i><a href="/wiki/Droungarios" title="Droungarios">droungarios</a></i> of the Byzantine <a href="/wiki/Theme_(Byzantine_district)" title="Theme (Byzantine district)">theme</a> of Thessalonica, and their mother's name was Maria.
</p><p>The exact ethnic origins of the brothers are unknown, there is controversy as to whether Cyril and Methodius were of <a href="/wiki/Early_Slavs" title="Early Slavs">Slavic</a><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> or <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> origin, or both.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The two brothers lost their father when Cyril was fourteen, and the powerful minister <a href="/wiki/Theoktistos" title="Theoktistos">Theoktistos</a>, who was <i><a href="/wiki/Logothetes_tou_dromou" title="Logothetes tou dromou">logothetes tou dromou</a></i>, one of the chief ministers of the Empire, became their protector. He was also responsible, along with the regent <a href="/wiki/Bardas" title="Bardas">Bardas</a>, for initiating a far-reaching educational program within the Empire which culminated in the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Magnaura" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Magnaura">University of Magnaura</a>, where Cyril was to teach. Cyril was ordained as <a href="/wiki/Priesthood_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Priesthood (Catholic Church)">priest</a> some time after his education, while his brother Methodius remained a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> until 867/868.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mission_to_the_Khazars">Mission to the Khazars</span></h3>
<p>About the year 860, <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Emperor">Byzantine Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Michael_III" title="Michael III">Michael III</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople">Patriarch of Constantinople</a> <a href="/wiki/Photios_I_of_Constantinople" title="Photios I of Constantinople">Photius</a> (a professor of Cyril's at the University and his guiding light in earlier years), sent Cyril on a missionary expedition to the <a href="/wiki/Khazars" title="Khazars">Khazars</a> who had requested a scholar be sent to them who could converse with both Jews and Saracens.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> It has been claimed that Methodius accompanied Cyril on the mission to the Khazars, but this may be a later invention.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The account of his life presented in the <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a> <i>"Legenda"</i> claims that he learned the Khazar language while in <a href="/wiki/Chersonesos_Taurica" class="mw-redirect" title="Chersonesos Taurica">Chersonesos</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Taurica" class="mw-redirect" title="Taurica">Taurica</a> (today <a href="/wiki/Crimea" title="Crimea">Crimea</a>).
</p><p>After his return to Constantinople, Cyril assumed the role of professor of <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophy</a> at the University while his brother had by this time become a significant figure in Byzantine political and administrative affairs, and an abbot of his monastery.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2009)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Mission_to_the_Slavs">Mission to the Slavs</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Great_Moravia">Great Moravia</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg/200px-Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="311" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg/300px-Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg/400px-Cyril_and_Methodius.jpg 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Cyril and Methodius, painting by <a href="/wiki/Jan_Matejko" title="Jan Matejko">Jan Matejko</a>, 1885</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 862, the brothers began the work which would give them their historical importance. That year Prince <a href="/wiki/Rastislav_of_Moravia" title="Rastislav of Moravia">Rastislav</a> of <a href="/wiki/Great_Moravia" title="Great Moravia">Great Moravia</a> requested that Emperor Michael III and the Patriarch Photius send missionaries to evangelize his Slavic subjects. His motives in doing so were probably more political than religious. Rastislav had become king with the support of the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Frankish</a> ruler <a href="/wiki/Louis_the_German" title="Louis the German">Louis the German</a>, but subsequently sought to assert his independence from the Franks. It is a common misconception that Cyril and Methodius were the first to bring Christianity to <a href="/wiki/Moravia" title="Moravia">Moravia</a>, but the letter from Rastislav to Michael III states clearly that Rastislav's people "had already rejected paganism and adhere to the Christian law."<sup id="cite_ref-Ivanov_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ivanov-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Rastislav is said to have expelled missionaries of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Church</a> and instead turned to Constantinople for ecclesiastical assistance and, presumably, a degree of political support.<sup id="cite_ref-Brit_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brit-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Emperor quickly chose to send Cyril, accompanied by his brother Methodius.<sup id="cite_ref-IUN_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IUN-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The request provided a convenient opportunity to expand Byzantine influence. Their first work seems to have been the training of assistants. In 863, they began the task of translating the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> and necessary <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_worship#Liturgical_books" title="Eastern Orthodox worship">liturgical books</a> into the language now known as <a href="/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic" title="Old Church Slavonic">Old Church Slavonic</a><sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_Encyclopedia_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic_Encyclopedia-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> and traveled to Great Moravia to promote it.<sup id="cite_ref-Pravmir_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pravmir-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> They enjoyed considerable success in this endeavour. However, they came into conflict with German ecclesiastics who opposed their efforts to create a specifically Slavic liturgy.
</p><p>For the purpose of this mission, they devised the <a href="/wiki/Glagolitic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Glagolitic alphabet">Glagolitic alphabet</a>, the first alphabet to be used for Slavonic manuscripts. The Glagolitic alphabet was suited to match the specific features of the Slavic language. Its descendant script, the Cyrillic, is still used by many languages today.<sup id="cite_ref-Brit_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brit-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The brothers wrote the first Slavic <a href="/wiki/Civil_Code" class="mw-redirect" title="Civil Code">Civil Code</a>, which was used in Great Moravia. The language derived from Old Church Slavonic, known as <a href="/wiki/Church_Slavonic" title="Church Slavonic">Church Slavonic</a>, is still used in <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgy</a> by several Orthodox Churches and also in some <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Catholic">Eastern Catholic</a> churches.
</p><p>It is impossible to determine with certainty exactly what the brothers translated. The <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Psalms" title="Psalms">Psalms</a> seem to have been the first, followed by other lessons from the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The <i>"Translatio"</i> speaks only of a version of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> by Cyril, and the <i>"Vita Methodii"</i> only of the <i>"evangelium Slovenicum,"</i> though other liturgical selections may also have been translated.
</p><p>Nor is it known for sure which liturgy, that of Rome or that of Constantinople, they took as a source. They may well have used the <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Roman alphabet</a>, as suggested by liturgical fragments which adhere closely to the Latin type. This view is confirmed by the "Prague Fragments" and by certain Old <a href="/wiki/Glagolitic" class="mw-redirect" title="Glagolitic">Glagolitic</a> liturgical fragments brought from <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kyiv</a> and discovered there by <a href="/wiki/Izmail_Sreznevsky" title="Izmail Sreznevsky">Izmail Sreznevsky</a>—probably the oldest document for the Slavonic tongue; these adhere closely to the Latin type, as is shown by the words <i>"Mass,"</i> <i>"Preface,"</i> and the name of one Felicitas. In any case, the circumstances were such that the brothers could hope for no permanent success without obtaining the authorization of Rome.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Journey_to_Rome">Journey to Rome</span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22">"Cyril and Methodius"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Cyril+and+Methodius%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">May 2019</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:San_clemente_fresco.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/200px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/300px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/San_clemente_fresco.jpg/400px-San_clemente_fresco.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2298" data-file-height="1615" /></a><figcaption>Saints Cyril and Methodius in Rome. Fresco in San Clemente</figcaption></figure>
<p>The mission of Constantine and Methodius had great success among Slavs in part because they used the people's native language rather than <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> or Greek. In Great Moravia, Constantine and Methodius also encountered missionaries from <a href="/wiki/East_Francia" title="East Francia">East Francia</a>, representing the western or Latin branch of the Church, and more particularly representing the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Empire" title="Carolingian Empire">Carolingian Empire</a> as founded by <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a>, and committed to linguistic, and cultural uniformity. They insisted on the use of the Latin liturgy, and they regarded Moravia and the Slavic peoples as part of their rightful mission field.
</p><p>When friction developed, the brothers, unwilling to be a cause of dissension among Christians, decided to travel to Rome to see the Pope, and seek a solution that would avoid quarreling between missionaries in the field. In 867, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_I" title="Pope Nicholas I">Pope Nicholas I</a> (858-867) invited the brothers to Rome. Their evangelizing mission in Moravia had by this time become the focus of a dispute with Archbishop <a href="/w/index.php?title=Adalwin_of_Salzburg&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Adalwin of Salzburg (page does not exist)">Adalwin of Salzburg</a> (859–873) and Bishop <a href="/wiki/Ermanrich,_Bishop_of_Passau" class="mw-redirect" title="Ermanrich, Bishop of Passau">Ermanrich</a> of <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Passau" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Passau">Passau</a> (866-874), who claimed ecclesiastical control of the same territory and wished to see it use the Latin liturgy exclusively.
</p><p>Travelling with the relics of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Clement I">Saint Clement</a> and a retinue of disciples, and passing through <a href="/wiki/Pannonia" title="Pannonia">Pannonia</a> (the <a href="/wiki/Balaton_Principality" class="mw-redirect" title="Balaton Principality">Balaton Principality</a>), where they were well received by Prince <a href="/wiki/Koce%C4%BE" class="mw-redirect" title="Koceľ">Koceľ</a>. This activity in Pannonia made a continuation of conflicts inevitable with the German episcopate, and especially with the bishop of <a href="/wiki/Salzburg" title="Salzburg">Salzburg</a>, to whose jurisdiction Pannonia had belonged for seventy-five years. As early as 865, Bishop Adalwin was found to exercise Episcopal rights there, and the administration under him was in the hands of the archpriest Riehbald. The latter was obliged to retire to Salzburg, but his superior was naturally disinclined to abandon his claims.
</p><p>The brothers sought support from Rome, and arrived there in 868, where they were warmly received. This was partly due to their bringing with them the relics of Saint Clement; the rivalry with Constantinople as to the jurisdiction over the territory of the Slavs would incline Rome to value the brothers and their influence.<sup id="cite_ref-Brit_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Brit-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The brothers were praised for their learning and cultivated for their influence in Constantinople. <a href="/wiki/Anastasius_Bibliothecarius" title="Anastasius Bibliothecarius">Anastasius Bibliothecarius</a> would later call Cyril "a man of apostolic life" and "a man of great wisdom".<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Their project in Moravia found support from the new <a href="/wiki/Pope_Adrian_II" title="Pope Adrian II">Pope Adrian II</a> (867-872), who formally authorized the use of the new Slavic liturgy. Subsequently, Methodius was ordained as priest by the pope himself, and five Slavic disciples were ordained as priests (<a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Gorazd&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Saint Gorazd (page does not exist)">Saint Gorazd</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Ohrid" title="Clement of Ohrid">Saint Clement of Ohrid</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Naum" title="Saint Naum">Saint Naum</a>) and as deacons (<a href="/wiki/Saint_Angelar" title="Saint Angelar">Saint Angelar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Sava_(disciple_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius)" title="Saint Sava (disciple of Saints Cyril and Methodius)">Saint Sava</a>) by the prominent bishops <a href="/wiki/Pope_Formosus" title="Pope Formosus">Formosus</a> and Gauderic.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Since the 10th century Cyril and Methodius along with these five disciples are collectively venerated by the <a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church" title="Bulgarian Orthodox Church">Bulgarian Orthodox Church</a> as the "<a href="/wiki/Seven_Apostles_of_Bulgarian_Orthodox_Church" title="Seven Apostles of Bulgarian Orthodox Church">Seven Saints</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The newly made priests officiated in their own languages at the altars of some of the principal churches. Feeling his end approaching, Cyril became a <a href="/wiki/Basilian_monks" title="Basilian monks">Basilian monk</a>, was given the new name Cyril,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and died in Rome fifty days later (14 February 869). There is some question as to assertion of the <i>Translatio</i> (ix.) that he was made a bishop. Upon Cyril´s death Methodius was given the title of Archbishop of <a href="/wiki/Sirmium" title="Sirmium">Sirmium</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Sremska_Mitrovica" title="Sremska Mitrovica">Sremska Mitrovica</a> in <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a>) with jurisdiction over all of Moravia and Pannonia, and authorisation to use the Slavonic Liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-Jugoslovenski_književni_leksikon_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jugoslovenski_književni_leksikon-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The statement of the <i>"Vita"</i> that Methodius was made bishop in 870 and not raised to the dignity of an archbishop until 873 is contradicted by the brief of <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_VIII" title="Pope John VIII">Pope John VIII</a>, written in June 879, according to which Adrian consecrated him archbishop; John includes in his jurisdiction not only Great Moravia and Pannonia, but Serbia as well.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Methodius_alone">Methodius alone</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanislav_Dospavski_-_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Stanislav_Dospavski_-_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius.png/170px-Stanislav_Dospavski_-_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/46/Stanislav_Dospavski_-_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius.png 1.5x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="399" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a> Cyril and Methodius by <a href="/wiki/Stanislav_Dospevski" title="Stanislav Dospevski">Stanislav Dospevski</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgarian</a> painter</figcaption></figure>
<p>Methodius now continued the work among the Slavs alone; not at first in Great Moravia, but in Pannonia (in the <a href="/wiki/Balaton_Principality" class="mw-redirect" title="Balaton Principality">Balaton Principality</a>), owing to the political circumstances of the former country, where Rastislav had been taken captive by his nephew <a href="/wiki/Svatopluk_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Svatopluk I">Svatopluk</a> in 870, then delivered over to <a href="/wiki/Carloman_of_Bavaria" title="Carloman of Bavaria">Carloman of Bavaria</a>, and condemned in a <a href="/wiki/Reichstag_(Holy_Roman_Empire)" class="mw-redirect" title="Reichstag (Holy Roman Empire)">diet</a> held at <a href="/wiki/Regensburg" title="Regensburg">Regensburg</a> at the end of 870. At the same time, the East Frankish rulers and their bishops decided to remove Methodius. The archiepiscopal claims of Methodius were considered such an injury to the rights of Salzburg that he was captured and forced to answer to East Frankish bishops: Adalwin of Salzburg, Ermanrich of Passau, and Anno of Freising. After a heated discussion, they declared the deposition of the intruder, and ordered him to be sent to Germany, where he was kept prisoner in a monastery for two and a half years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBowlus1995165-186_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowlus1995165-186-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In spite of the strong representations of the <i><a href="/wiki/Conversio_Bagoariorum_et_Carantanorum" title="Conversio Bagoariorum et Carantanorum">Conversio Bagoariorum et Carantanorum</a></i>, written in 871 to influence the pope, though not avowing this purpose, Rome declared emphatically for Methodius, and sent a bishop, Paul of <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Ancona" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Ancona">Ancona</a>, to reinstate him and punish his enemies, after which both parties were commanded to appear in Rome with the legate.
Thus in 873, new <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_VIII" title="Pope John VIII">Pope John VIII</a> (872-882) secured the release of Methodius, but instructed him to stop using the Slavonic Liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGoldberg2006319-320_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldberg2006319-320-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Methodius.27_final_years"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Methodius'_final_years">Methodius' final years</span></h3>
<p>The papal will prevailed, and Methodius secured his freedom and his archiepiscopal authority over both Great Moravia and Pannonia, though the use of Slavonic for the mass was still denied to him. His authority was restricted in Pannonia when after Koceľ's death the principality was administered by German nobles; but <a href="/wiki/Svatopluk_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Svatopluk I">Svatopluk</a> now ruled with practical independence in Great Moravia, and expelled the German clergy. This apparently secured an undisturbed field of operation for Methodius, and the <i>Vita</i> (x.) depicts the next few years (873–879) as a period of fruitful progress. Methodius seems to have disregarded, wholly or in part, the prohibition of the Slavonic liturgy; and when <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Frankish</a> clerics again found their way into the country, and the archbishop's strictness had displeased the licentious Svatopluk, this was made a cause of complaint against him at Rome, coupled with charges regarding the <i><a href="/wiki/Filioque_clause" class="mw-redirect" title="Filioque clause">Filioque</a></i>.
</p><p>In 878, Methodius was summoned to Rome on charges of heresy and using Slavonic. This time Pope John was convinced by the arguments that Methodius made in his defence and sent him back cleared of all charges, and with permission to use Slavonic. The Carolingian bishop who succeeded him, Witching, suppressed the Slavonic Liturgy and forced the followers of Methodius into exile. Many found refuge with Knyaz <a href="/wiki/Boris_I_of_Bulgaria" title="Boris I of Bulgaria">Boris the Baptizer</a> in <a href="/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire" title="First Bulgarian Empire">Bulgaria</a>, under whom they reorganized a Slavic-speaking Church. Meanwhile, Pope John's successors adopted a Latin-only policy which lasted for centuries.
</p><p>Methodius vindicated his orthodoxy at Rome, the more easily as the creed was still recited there without the <i>Filioque</i>, and promised to obey in regard to the liturgy. The other party was conciliated by giving him a <a href="/wiki/Swabia" title="Swabia">Swabian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wiching" title="Wiching">Wiching</a>, as his coadjutor. When relations were strained between the two, John VIII steadfastly supported Methodius; but after his death (December 882) the archbishop's position became insecure, and his need of support induced Goetz to accept the statement of the <i>Vita</i> (xiii.) that he went to visit the Eastern emperor.
</p><p>It was not until after Methodius' death, which is placed on 6 April 885,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> that the animosity erupted into an open conflict. Gorazd, whom Methodius had designated as his successor, was not recognised by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Stephen_V" title="Pope Stephen V">Pope Stephen V</a>. The same Pope forbade the use of the Slavic liturgy<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> and placed the infamous Wiching as Methodius' successor. The latter exiled the disciples of the two brothers from Great Moravia in 885. They fled to the <a href="/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire" title="First Bulgarian Empire">First Bulgarian Empire</a>, where they were welcomed and commissioned to establish two theological schools - the <a href="/wiki/Ohrid_Literary_School" title="Ohrid Literary School">Ohrid Literary School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ohrid" title="Ohrid">Ohrid</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Preslav_Literary_School" title="Preslav Literary School">Preslav Literary School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Veliki_Preslav" title="Veliki Preslav">Preslav</a> which was originally established in <a href="/wiki/Pliska" title="Pliska">Pliska</a> and was moved to Preslav in 893. In the school in Preslav they<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> devised the <a href="/wiki/Cyrillic_script" title="Cyrillic script">Cyrillic script</a> on the basis of the <a href="/wiki/Greek_alphabet" title="Greek alphabet">Greek</a> and <a href="/wiki/Glagolitic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Glagolitic alphabet">Glagolitic</a> alphabets. Cyrillic gradually replaced Glagolitic as the alphabet of the Old Church Slavonic language, which became the official language of the <a href="/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire" title="First Bulgarian Empire">First Bulgarian Empire</a> and later spread to the Eastern Slav lands of <a href="/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27" title="Kievan Rus&#39;">Kievan Rus'</a>. Cyrillic eventually spread throughout most of the Slavic world to become the standard alphabet in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> Slavic countries. Hence, the efforts of Cyril and Methodius and their disciples also paved the way for the spread of Christianity throughout Eastern Europe.
</p><p>Methodius' body was buried in the main cathedral church of Great Moravia. Until today it remains an open question which city was capital of Great Moravia and therefore the place of Methodius' eternal rest remains unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Invention_of_the_Glagolitic_and_Cyrillic_alphabets">Invention of the Glagolitic and Cyrillic alphabets</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bascanska_ploca.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Bascanska_ploca.jpg/200px-Bascanska_ploca.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="113" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Bascanska_ploca.jpg/300px-Bascanska_ploca.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Bascanska_ploca.jpg/400px-Bascanska_ploca.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="509" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Ba%C5%A1ka_tablet" title="Baška tablet">Baška tablet</a> is an early example of the Glagolitic from <a href="/wiki/Croatia" title="Croatia">Croatia</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon,_1938.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg/220px-Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg/330px-Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg/440px-Cyril_and_Methodius_-_Bulgarian_cartoon%2C_1938.jpg 2x" data-file-width="684" data-file-height="744" /></a><figcaption>A cartoon about Saints Cyril and Methodius from Bulgaria in 1938. The caption reads&#160;: <i>Brother Cyril, go tell those who are inside to learn the alphabet so they know freedom (<a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_language" title="Bulgarian language">Bulgarian</a>: <span lang="bg">свобода</span>) and anarchy (<a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_language" title="Bulgarian language">Bulgarian</a>: <span lang="bg">слободия</span>) are not the same.</i></figcaption></figure>
<p>The Glagolitic and Cyrillic alphabets are the oldest known Slavic <a href="/wiki/Alphabet" title="Alphabet">alphabets</a>, and were created by the two brothers and/or their students, to translate the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">Gospels</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_worship#Liturgical_books" title="Eastern Orthodox worship">liturgical books</a><sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_Encyclopedia_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic_Encyclopedia-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> into the <a href="/wiki/Slavic_languages" title="Slavic languages">Slavic languages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-BritGlago_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritGlago-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The early Glagolitic alphabet was used in Great Moravia between 863 (the arrival of Cyril and Methodius) and 885 (the expulsion of their students) for government and religious documents and books, and at the Great Moravian Academy (<i>Veľkomoravské učilište</i>) founded by Cyril, where followers of Cyril and Methodius were educated, by Methodius himself among others. The alphabet has been traditionally attributed to Cyril. That attribution has been confirmed explicitly by the papal letter <i>Industriae tuae</i> (880) approving the use of Old Church Slavonic, which says that the alphabet was "invented by Constantine the Philosopher". The term invention need not exclude the possibility of the brothers having made use of earlier letters, but implies only that before that time the Slavic languages had no distinct script of their own.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Early_Cyrillic_alphabet" title="Early Cyrillic alphabet">early Cyrillic alphabet</a> was developed by the disciples of Saints Cyril and Methodius at the <a href="/wiki/Preslav_Literary_School" title="Preslav Literary School">Preslav Literary School</a> (previously in <a href="/wiki/Pliska" title="Pliska">Pliska</a> as <a href="/wiki/Pliska_Literary_School" class="mw-redirect" title="Pliska Literary School">Pliska Literary School</a>) at the end of the 9th century as a simplification of the Glagolitic alphabet which more closely resembled the <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a> alphabet.
</p><p>After the death of Cyril, <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Ohrid" title="Clement of Ohrid">Clement of Ohrid</a> accompanied Methodius from Rome to Pannonia and Great Moravia. After the death of Methodius in 885, Clement headed the struggle against the German clergy in Great Moravia along with <a href="/w/index.php?title=Saint_Gorazd&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Saint Gorazd (page does not exist)">Gorazd</a>. After spending some time in jail, he was expelled from Great Moravia, and in 885 or 886 reached the borders of the Bulgarian Empire together with <a href="/wiki/Saint_Naum" title="Saint Naum">Naum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Angelar" title="Saint Angelar">Angelar</a>, and possibly Gorazd (according to other sources, Gorazd was already dead by that time). Clement and Naum were afterwards sent to the Bulgarian capital of Pliska, where they were commissioned by <a href="/wiki/Boris_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Boris I">Boris I</a> to instruct the future clergy of the state in the Slavonic language.
</p><p>After the adoption of Christianity in 865, religious ceremonies in Bulgaria were conducted in Greek by clergy sent from the Byzantine Empire. Fearing growing Byzantine influence and weakening of the state, Boris viewed the adoption of the Old Slavonic language as a way to preserve the political independence and stability of Bulgaria, so he established two literary schools (academies), in <a href="/wiki/Pliska" title="Pliska">Pliska</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ohrid" title="Ohrid">Ohrid</a>, where theology was to be taught in the Slavonic language. While <a href="/wiki/Naum_of_Preslav" class="mw-redirect" title="Naum of Preslav">Naum of Preslav</a> stayed in Pliska working on the foundation of the <a href="/wiki/Pliska_Literary_School" class="mw-redirect" title="Pliska Literary School">Pliska Literary School</a>, Clement was commissioned by Boris I to organise the teaching of theology to future clergymen in Old Church Slavonic at the <a href="/wiki/Ohrid_Literary_School" title="Ohrid Literary School">Ohrid Literary School</a>. For seven years (886-893) Clement taught some 3,500 students in the Slavonic language and the Glagolitic alphabet.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Commemoration">Commemoration</span></h2>
<h3><span id="Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius.27_Day"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius'_Day">Saints Cyril and Methodius' Day</span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg/250px-Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg/375px-Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg/500px-Cross_Procession_in_Novosibirsk_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption>Saints Cyril and Methodius procession</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonization</a> process was much more relaxed in the decades following Cyril's death than today. Cyril was regarded by his disciples as a saint soon after his death. His following spread among the nations he evangelized and subsequently to the wider Christian Church, and he was famous as a holy man, along with his brother Methodius. There were calls for Cyril's canonization from the crowds lining the Roman streets during his funeral procession. The brothers' first appearance in a papal document is in <i>Grande Munus</i> of Leo XIII in 1880. They are known as the "Apostles of the Slavs", and are still highly regarded by both Roman Catholic and Orthodox Christians. Their <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is currently celebrated on 14 February in the Roman Catholic Church (to coincide with the date of St Cyril's death); on 11 May in the Eastern Orthodox Church (though for Eastern Orthodox Churches which use the <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a> this is 24 May according to the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a>); and on 7 July according to the old sanctoral calendar that existed before the revisions of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a>. The celebration also commemorates the introduction of literacy and the preaching of the gospels in the Slavonic language by the brothers. The brothers were declared "Patrons of Europe" in 1980.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first recorded secular celebration of Saints Cyril and Methodius' Day as the "Day of the Bulgarian script", as traditionally accepted by <a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_history" class="mw-redirect" title="Bulgarian history">Bulgarian history</a>, was held in the town of <a href="/wiki/Plovdiv" title="Plovdiv">Plovdiv</a> on 11 May 1851, when a local Bulgarian school was named "Saints Cyril and Methodius": both acts on the initiative of the prominent Bulgarian educator <a href="/wiki/Nayden_Gerov" title="Nayden Gerov">Nayden Gerov</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> although an Armenian traveller mentioned his visit to the "celebration of the Bulgarian script" in the town of <a href="/wiki/Shumen" title="Shumen">Shumen</a> on 22 May 1803.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cyril and Methodius are <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and with a lesser feast on the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church calendar</a><sup id="cite_ref-teccalendar_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-teccalendar-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> on <a href="/wiki/February_14" title="February 14">14 February</a>.
</p><p>The day is now celebrated as a <a href="/wiki/Public_holiday" title="Public holiday">public holiday</a> in the following countries:
</p>
<ul><li>In <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a> it is celebrated on 24 May and is known as the "Bulgarian Education and Culture, and Slavonic Script Day" (Bulgarian: Ден на българската просвета и култура и на славянската писменост), a <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Bulgaria" title="Public holidays in Bulgaria">national holiday</a> celebrating <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Bulgaria" title="Culture of Bulgaria">Bulgarian culture</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_literature" title="Bulgarian literature">literature</a> as well as the alphabet. It is also known as "Alphabet, Culture, and Education Day" (Bulgarian: Ден на азбуката, културата и просвещението). Saints Cyril and Methodius are patrons of the National Library of Bulgaria. There is a monument to them in front of the library. Saints Cyril and Methodius are the most celebrated saints in the Bulgarian Orthodox church, and icons of the two brothers can be found in every church.</li>
<li>In <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia" title="North Macedonia">North Macedonia</a>, it is celebrated on 24 May and is known as the "Saints Cyril and Methodius, Slavonic Enlighteners' Day" (<a href="/wiki/Macedonian_language" title="Macedonian language">Macedonian</a>: <span lang="mk">Св. Кирил и Методиј, Ден на словенските просветители</span>), a <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_North_Macedonia" title="Public holidays in North Macedonia">national holiday</a>. The Government of the Republic of Macedonia enacted a statute of the national holiday in October 2006 and the Parliament of the Republic of Macedonia passed a corresponding law at the beginning of 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Previously it had only been celebrated in the schools. It is also known as the day of the "<a href="/wiki/Thessaloniki" title="Thessaloniki">Solun</a> Brothers" (<a href="/wiki/Macedonian_language" title="Macedonian language">Macedonian</a>: <span lang="mk">Солунските браќа</span>).</li>
<li>In the <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a>, the two brothers were originally commemorated on 9 March, but <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a> changed this date to 5 July for several reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-CMHoliday_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CMHoliday-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Today, Saints Cyril and Methodius are revered there as national saints and their name day (5 July), "Sts Cyril and Methodius Day" is a <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_the_Czech_Republic" title="Public holidays in the Czech Republic">national holiday in Czech Republic</a> and <a href="/wiki/National_holidays_in_Slovakia" class="mw-redirect" title="National holidays in Slovakia">Slovakia</a>. In the Czech Republic it is celebrated as "Slavic Missionaries Cyril and Methodius Day" (Czech: Den slovanských věrozvěstů Cyrila a Metoděje); in <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a> it is celebrated as "St. Cyril and Metod Day" (Slovak: Sviatok svätého Cyrila a Metoda).<sup id="cite_ref-CMHoliday_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CMHoliday-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, it is celebrated on 24 May and is known as the "Slavonic Literature and Culture Day" (Russian: День славянской письменности и культуры), celebrating Slavonic culture and literature as well as the alphabet. Its celebration is ecclesiastical (11 May in the Church's <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian calendar</a>). It is not a public holiday in Russia.</li></ul>
<p>The saints' <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is celebrated by the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> on 11 May and by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a> on 14 February as "<a href="/wiki/Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Saints Cyril and Methodius Day">Saints Cyril and Methodius Day</a>". The <a href="/wiki/Lutheranism" title="Lutheranism">Lutheran Churches</a> of Western Christianity commemorate the two saints either on 14 February or 11 May. The <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite_Lutheranism" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Rite Lutheranism">Byzantine Rite Lutheran Churches</a> celebrate Saints Cyril and Methodius Day on 24 May.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_commemoration">Other commemoration</span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/SS._Cyril_and_Methodius_National_Library" title="SS. Cyril and Methodius National Library">The national library of Bulgaria</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sofia" title="Sofia">Sofia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ss._Cyril_and_Methodius_University_of_Skopje" title="Ss. Cyril and Methodius University of Skopje">Ss. Cyril and Methodius University of Skopje</a> in the <a href="/wiki/North_Macedonia" title="North Macedonia">North Macedonia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Veliko_Tarnovo_University" title="Veliko Tarnovo University">St. Cyril and St. Methodius University of Veliko Tarnovo</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bulgaria" title="Bulgaria">Bulgaria</a> and in <a href="/wiki/Trnava" title="Trnava">Trnava</a>, <a href="/wiki/Slovakia" title="Slovakia">Slovakia</a>, bear the name of the two saints. Faculty of Theology at <a href="/wiki/Palack%C3%BD_University,_Olomouc" class="mw-redirect" title="Palacký University, Olomouc">Palacký University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Olomouc" title="Olomouc">Olomouc</a> (<a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a>), bears the name <i>"Saints Cyril and Methodius Faculty of Theology"</i>. In the United States, <a href="/wiki/SS._Cyril_and_Methodius_Seminary" title="SS. Cyril and Methodius Seminary">SS. Cyril and Methodius Seminary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Orchard_Lake,_Michigan" class="mw-redirect" title="Orchard Lake, Michigan">Orchard Lake, Michigan</a>, bears their name.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Brotherhood_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius" title="Brotherhood of Saints Cyril and Methodius">Brotherhood of Saints Cyril and Methodius</a> established in 1846 was short-lived a pro-Ukrainian organization in the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> to preserve Ukrainian national identity.
</p><p>Saints Cyril and Methodius are the main patron saints of the <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Ljubljana" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Ljubljana">Archdiocese of Ljubljana</a>. <a href="/wiki/Ljubljana_Cathedral" title="Ljubljana Cathedral">Ljubljana Cathedral</a> stands at <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cyril_and_Methodius_Square&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cyril and Methodius Square (page does not exist)">Cyril and Methodius Square</a> (<a href="/wiki/Slovene_language" title="Slovene language">Slovene</a>: <i lang="sl">Ciril–Metodov trg</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> They are also patron saints of the <a href="/wiki/Slovak_Catholic_Eparchy_of_Ko%C5%A1ice" title="Slovak Catholic Eparchy of Košice">Greek-Catholic Eparchy of Košice</a> (Slovakia)<sup id="cite_ref-grkatke-hist_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-grkatke-hist-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Eparchy_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius_of_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="Eparchy of Saints Cyril and Methodius of Toronto">Slovak Greek Catholic Eparchy of Toronto</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/St._Cyril_Peak" title="St. Cyril Peak">St. Cyril Peak</a> and <a href="/wiki/St._Methodius_Peak" title="St. Methodius Peak">St. Methodius Peak</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Tangra_Mountains" title="Tangra Mountains">Tangra Mountains</a> on <a href="/wiki/Livingston_Island" title="Livingston Island">Livingston Island</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Shetland_Islands" title="South Shetland Islands">South Shetland Islands</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Antarctica" title="Antarctica">Antarctica</a> are named for the brothers.
</p><p>Saint Cyril's remains are interred in a shrine-chapel within the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Clemente" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica di San Clemente">Basilica di San Clemente</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>. The chapel holds a Madonna by <a href="/wiki/Sassoferrato" title="Sassoferrato">Sassoferrato</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius_(Danville,_Pennsylvania)" title="Basilica of Saints Cyril and Methodius (Danville, Pennsylvania)">Basilica of SS. Cyril and Methodius</a> in <a href="/wiki/Danville,_Pennsylvania" title="Danville, Pennsylvania">Danville, Pennsylvania</a>, (the only <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">basilica</a> dedicated to SS. Cyril and Methodius in the world) is the motherhouse chapel of the Sisters of SS. Cyril and Methodius, a Roman Catholic women's religious community of <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_right" title="Pontifical right">pontifical right</a> dedicated to apostolic works of ecumenism, education, evangelization, and elder care.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saints_Cyril_and_Methodius" title="Order of Saints Cyril and Methodius">Order of Saints Cyril and Methodius</a>, originally founded in 1909, is part of the national award system of Bulgaria.
</p><p>In 2021, a <a href="/wiki/Research_vessel" title="Research vessel">research vessel</a> newly acquired by the <a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_Navy" title="Bulgarian Navy">Bulgarian Navy</a> was <a href="/wiki/Ship_christening" class="mw-redirect" title="Ship christening">re-christened</a> <i>Ss. Cyril and Methodius</i> after the saints, with actress <a href="/wiki/Maria_Bakalova" title="Maria Bakalova">Maria Bakalova</a> as the <a href="/wiki/Ship_sponsor" title="Ship sponsor">sponsor</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Names_in_other_relevant_languages">Names in other relevant languages</span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Κύριλλος καὶ Μεθόδιος</span> (<i>Kýrillos kaí Methódios</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic" title="Old Church Slavonic">Old Church Slavonic</a>: Кѷриллъ и Меѳодїи</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Belarusian_language" title="Belarusian language">Belarusian</a>: <span lang="be">Кірыла і Мяфодзій</span> (<i>Kiryła i Miafodzij</i>) or <span title="Belarusian-language text"><span lang="be">Кірыла і Мятода</span></span> (<i>Kiryła i Miatoda</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_language" title="Bulgarian language">Bulgarian</a>: <span lang="bg">Кирил и Методий</span> (<i>Kiril i Metodiy</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Croatian_language" title="Croatian language">Croatian</a>: <i lang="hr">Ćiril i Metod</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Czech_language" title="Czech language">Czech</a>: <i lang="cs">Cyril a Metoděj</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Macedonian_language" title="Macedonian language">Macedonian</a>: <span lang="mk">Кирил и Методиј</span> (<i>Kiril i Metodij</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Church_Slavonic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Church Slavonic language">New Church Slavonic</a>: Кѷрі́ллъ и҆ Меѳо́дїй (<i>Kỳrill" i Methodij</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Polish_language" title="Polish language">Polish</a>: <i lang="pl">Cyryl i Metody</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Romanian_language" title="Romanian language">Romanian</a>: <i lang="ro">Chiril și Metodie</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">Кири́лл и Мефодий</span> (<i>Kirill i Mefodij</i>), pre-1918 spelling: <span title="Russian-language text"><span lang="ru">Кириллъ и Меѳодій</span></span> (<i>Kirill" i Methodij</i>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Serbian_language" title="Serbian language">Serbian</a>: <span lang="sr">Ћирило и Методије</span> / <span title="Serbian-language text"><i lang="sr-Latn">Ćirilo i Metodije</i></span></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Slovak_language" title="Slovak language">Slovak</a>: <i lang="sk">Cyril a Metod</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Slovene_language" title="Slovene language">Slovene</a>: <i lang="sl">Ciril in Metod</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_language" title="Ukrainian language">Ukrainian</a>: <span lang="uk">Кирило і Мефодій</span> (<i>Kyrylo i Mefodij</i>)</li></ul>

Andrews McMeel Publishing, 2003. p. 37. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780740733369" title="Special:BookSources/9780740733369">9780740733369</a></li></ul>
 ...Cyril was born of Greek nobility connected with the senate of Thessalonica, although his mother may have been of Slavic descent...

<ul><li>2. <i>UNESCO Features: A Fortnightly Press Service</i>. United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization., 1984. University of Michigan</li></ul>
 ...They may have been of wholly Slavic descent or of mixed Greco-Slav origin...</span>
</li>
